## Reasoning

The champion uses: State 0 = Spiral (spd=15), transitions to state 2 when perceiving color 4 (yellow). State 1 = Exploration (rwm=31), transitions to state 0 when neighbors < 3. State 2 = GoAwayColor (clr=4, yellow), transitions to state 1 (index 3 in 3-state relative addressing means... wait, let me re-read).

Actually with 3 states, from state 2 the others are [0, 1], so D=0→state0, D=1→state1, D=2→... wait, with 3 states leaving state 2, others are [0,1]: D=0→state0, D=1→state1. But `--n2x0 3` means D=3? That seems invalid. Let me just keep the champion verbatim.

The key insight: robots need to go TOWARD BLUE walls (color 6 = cyan? or which color is blue?). Looking at the color IDs: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. So BLUE walls = color 3 (blue). GoToColor with clr=3 should navigate toward blue walls.

**Strategy:**
- State doing GoToColor blue (clr=3) to navigate toward upper strip
- Once on black floor, stop or stay
- Combine with GoAwayColor green (clr=2) as alternative

Let me design 14 machines:

1. **Champion** (verbatim)
2-6. **Exploitation** of champion - small tweaks
7-14. **Exploration** - GoToColor blue, GoAwayColor green combinations

## Reasoning

The champion (score 2310.98) uses a 3-state FSM:
- State 0: GoAwayColor green (clr=2) → go away from green walls. Transitions: perceive blue (color 3) → go to state 1; random 0.1 → state 1.
- State 1: GoToColor blue (clr=3) → move toward blue walls. Transitions: black floor → state 1 (self? no - D=1 means state 2 since others are [0,2]); random 0.05 → state 0.

Wait, let me re-read. With 3 states, leaving state 1, others are [0, 2]: D=0→state0, D=1→state2.
- State 1 transition 0: D=1→state2 on black floor (p=0.8)
- State 1 transition 1: D=0→state0 on random (p=0.05)
- State 2: Stop. Transitions: D=1→state2? No, D=0→state0 on gray floor (p=0.9), D=1→... wait with 3 states leaving state 2, others are [0,1]: D=0→state0, D=1→state1.
- State 2 (Stop): go to state1(=state1) on gray floor (p=0.9), go to state0(=state0) on random (p=0.02)

So the logic: flee green → see blue → go to blue → on black floor stop → if gray floor detected, go back to GoToBlue.

This is excellent! The key insight: GoAwayGreen + GoToBlue + Stop on black floor.

**Exploitation strategy:**
1. Champion verbatim
2-6. Tweak probabilities slightly (p0x0: 0.65-0.75, p0x1: 0.05-0.15, p1x0: 0.7-0.9, p1x1: 0.03-0.07)
7-9. Swap state order (GoToBlue first, then GoAwayGreen)
10-11. Add 4th state for more nuance
12-14. Different topologies

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (1733.73) has a clear structure:
- **State 0**: GoAwayColor green (clr=2) → flee green walls. If perceive blue → go to state 1; random p=0.1 → state 1.
- **State 1**: GoToColor blue (clr=3) → move toward blue walls. If black floor (p=0.9) → stop (state 2); random p=0.03 → back to state 0.
- **State 2**: Stop. If gray floor (p=0.9) → state 1; random p=0.02 → state 0; if perceive blue (p=0.3) → state 1.

The third transition in state 2 (perceive blue → go to GoToBlue) is what differentiates the champion from the 2nd-best. This is a key insight: while stopped, if blue is still visible, re-engage GoToBlue.

**Exploitation**: Fine-tune probabilities around the champion's values:
- p0x0 (perceive blue trigger): try 0.6-0.8
- p0x1 (random escape): try 0.05-0.15
- p1x0 (black floor → stop): try 0.85-0.95
- p1x1 (random → flee green): try 0.02-0.05
- p2x0 (gray → GoToBlue): try 0.85-0.95
- p2x2 (perceive blue while stopped): try 0.2-0.5

**Exploration**: Try 4-state versions, different color triggers, adding neighbor-count conditions.

## Reasoning

The champion (1406.52) key features:
- State 0: GoAwayColor green (clr=2). Transitions: see blue → state1 (p=0.7); **black floor** → state1 (p=0.6). The black floor trigger in state 0 is new vs previous champion - it helps robots that reach any black floor to immediately try GoToBlue.
- State 1: GoToColor blue (clr=3). Transitions: black floor → stop/state2 (p=0.9); random → flee/state0 (p=0.03).
- State 2: Stop. Transitions: gray floor → GoToBlue/state1 (p=0.9); random → flee/state0 (p=0.02); see blue → GoToBlue/state1 (p=0.3).

Key insight: The champion uses **black floor condition (C=0)** in state 0 as a second trigger (p=0.6) alongside perceive blue. This is what distinguishes it.

**Exploitation**: Vary p0x1 (black floor trigger: 0.4-0.8), p0x0 (perceive blue: 0.6-0.8), p1x0 (black→stop: 0.85-0.95), p2x2 (perceive blue while stopped: 0.2-0.5).

**Exploration**: Try combining the 4-transition stop state (from 2nd best) with the champion's state 0 structure; try adding cyan/magenta perception; try different floor conditions.

Note: Valid LED colors are {0,4,5,6}. Colors for C=7: {1=red,2=green,3=blue,4=yellow,5=magenta,6=cyan}.

## Reasoning

The champion (1406.52) has been stable. The top performers are all very close in score (1406-1479), suggesting we're near a local optimum. Key parameters to tune:

- **p0x0** (perceive blue → GoToBlue): best performers use 0.7-0.8
- **p0x1** (black floor → GoToBlue from state 0): best use 0.55-0.6
- **p1x0** (black floor → stop): best use 0.9-0.93
- **p1x1** (random → flee green): best use 0.02-0.03
- **p2x0** (gray → GoToBlue): best use 0.9-0.95
- **p2x1** (random → flee green): best use 0.01-0.02
- **p2x2** (perceive blue while stopped → GoToBlue): best use 0.15-0.45

The 4-state version was worse. Adding extra transitions to state 0 was also worse. The core 3-state structure with exactly these two transitions in state 0 and three in state 2 is the winner.

Let me focus exploitation tightly around the best parameters, and add some exploration with different stop-state behaviors.

## Reasoning

The champion (1406.52) has been stable for 3 rounds. We're converging around a narrow parameter range. Key observations:

- The state 2 "stop" transition D=1 (→state1 GoToBlue) on gray floor works well; D=0 (→state0 GoAwayGreen) means back to fleeing. The worst performers confirm: using C=0 (black floor) instead of C=1 (gray floor) in state2 tx0 is bad, and having state2 go to state0 (D=0) for perceive-blue is bad.
- p0x0 (blue perception trigger): 0.70-0.82 range, sweet spot ~0.70-0.76
- p0x1 (black floor trigger): 0.52-0.65, sweet spot ~0.58-0.62
- p1x0 (black→stop): 0.90-0.95, sweet spot ~0.90-0.93
- p2x2 (blue-while-stopped→GoToBlue): 0.18-0.45, sweet spot ~0.28-0.35

For exploration, try: slightly different p2x2 values, try adding a white floor condition, try swapping state 2 to a slow exploration instead of full stop.

## Reasoning

The champion has held at 1406.52 for 4 rounds. We're clearly in a tight local optimum. The best performers all share the same topology but differ slightly in parameters. Let me analyze what's working:

- Best p0x0 range: ~0.70-0.76 (perceive blue → GoToBlue)
- Best p0x1 range: ~0.58-0.62 (black floor → GoToBlue from flee state)
- Best p1x0 range: ~0.90-0.93 (black floor → stop)
- Best p1x1 range: ~0.02-0.03 (random → flee)
- Best p2x0 range: ~0.90-0.93 (gray → GoToBlue)
- Best p2x1 range: ~0.015-0.025 (random → flee)
- Best p2x2 range: ~0.22-0.30 (perceive blue while stopped → GoToBlue)

The 0.27 value in best performer #1 is slightly below champion's 0.30. Let me try combinations around these sweet spots more carefully, and a few genuine explorations like trying cyan (color 6) walls or neighbor-count conditions to prevent drift.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
OBJECTIVE. At every simulation step the loop function adds +1 to the score for each robot that is NOT inside the target region. The score is MINIMISED, so a lower score means the swarm reached the region earlier and more robots stayed in it.

THE TARGET REGION is the upper strip of the arena. It is NOT identified by a unique floor colour: the floor is BLACK in BOTH the upper and the lower strip, and GRAY in between. Only the UPPER black strip is rewarded. Sitting on the lower black strip scores exactly as badly as standing in the middle of the arena.

HOW TO TELL THE TWO STRIPS APART. At the start of the run the arena walls light up: the two walls bordering the REWARDED upper strip are BLUE, the two walls bordering the penalised lower strip are GREEN. Colour is the only reliable cue.

CONSEQUENCE FOR THE FSM. A black-floor condition is useful to detect that a strip has been reached, but on its own it sends half the swarm to the wrong strip. It must be combined with colour-driven motion - move toward BLUE, or move away from GREEN - to reach the correct one. Once inside, robots should stay rather than keep exploring, since every step spent outside costs a point. Clustering tightly is NOT rewarded: only being inside counts.

# 2. THE ARENA FOR THIS RUN
The arena is a regular polygon with 6 walls, inner radius 0.85 m. 14 e-puck robots start scattered uniformly in a square of half-width 0.50 m around the centre, with random orientations. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.49 m: neighbour-count conditions only see robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).
For reference, the published setup uses 20 robots and a range of 0.50 m, so thresholds tuned for that setup may need adjusting here.

# 3. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 4. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 5. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 6. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
